172.

Following these come the noble and the ignoble, who likewise commit whoredom <term>whoredom: the author uses "Hurerey" (harlotry/whoredom) as a metaphor for any desire or attachment that takes the place of God in a person's heart</term>; for there is no one who is without flaw. There is such an abomination and whoredom on land and water among the rich and poor, among the learned and unlearned, among the wise and unwise, that it cannot be sufficiently written about. One loves beautiful horses, another beautiful dogs, another exotic animals <term>exotic animals: literally "strange beasts," referring to the keeping of rare animals as a status symbol</term>; one loves art, another clothing, another pride <term>pride: original "Hoffarth," meaning vanity, pomp, or social grandiosity</term>, another banquets, another houses and castles, another many fields, another much money. (Note well) <note>original: "NB." for *nota bene*</note>. One loves many books; one likes this, another likes that. I say, it is impossible to recount it all—what kind of whoredom is committed with a burning spirit and great desire in the world by all people before they satisfy their lust-sickness and obtain what they desire. Once they have attained it, the sin is committed, and they do not let it rest there; just as a whore does not leave her whoredom, so they cannot cease from their desires and cravings.

Now see, dear person, all such things are punished by God as whoredom, and are regarded and named as such. For He created man free and upright; he should possess nothing with [lustful] desire, but rather if he has something, let it be as if he did not have it; if he knows something, let it be as if he did not know it <note>This reflects a common mystical theme of "detachment" (*Gelassenheit*), where one uses worldly things without becoming spiritually attached to them</note>. For God will give His glory to no other. But because we take it for ourselves and wish to possess it as our own property, she shall fall—the great Babylon—and she shall become a dwelling place for evil spirits. Oh, I have said far too little of this whore, for the mystery <term>mystery: original "Geheimnus," referring to a hidden spiritual reality or a divine secret</term> is too great. Know that this whore sits and reigns in heaven and also on earth, for it is written: *And I saw the woman drunk with the blood of the saints, and with the blood of the witnesses of Jesus* <note>original: "Und Ich sahe das Weib truncken von dem Blut der Heyligen / unnd von dem Blut der Zeugen Jesu," a reference to Revelation 17:6</note>. <summary>This page features a highly structured mystical table ("Tabula") that synchronizes verses from Hebrews and Revelation with the three ages of sacred history: the Old Testament, New Testament, and the present world. It uses the symbolic creatures (Lion, Ox, Eagle, Angel/Man) and the Trinity to map out God's progressive revelation to humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Revelation, Old Testament, New Testament, Alpha and Omega, Dragon, Seven Churches, Four Living Creatures, Mysticism</keywords>

### Ulrich Guide of Utopia. <note>original: *Vldaricus Wegweiser Vtopiensis*. This is a pseudonym; "Wegweiser" means "signpost" or "guide," and "Utopiensis" refers to the fictional land of Utopia, suggesting the author wished to remain anonymous or was writing a satirical or visionary work.</note>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page contains a liturgical text, likely from a Missal or a Pontifical (a bishop's service book). It combines a prayer with instructions for the ordination of minor clergy and a scripture reading from the Old Testament.</meta>

God, who we perceive to cause ancient miracles to shine forth even in the present age <note>original: "Deus qui antiqua miracula in p[re]senti quoque s[e]c[u]lo coruscare sentimus"</note>, grant, we beseech you: that the mystery by which you delivered your people from the Egyptians for their restoration <note>This prayer connects the historical Exodus of the Israelites from Egypt with the spiritual "liberation" found in the Christian rite of baptism.</note> may now be worked for the salvation of the nations through the waters of <term>baptism: a ritual of purification and initiation into the church</term>. Through our Lord.

**Then let him ordain the readers: and when they have been ordained,** <note>The "readers" (or lectors) were a specific rank of junior clergy in the medieval church whose primary duty was to read the scriptures during the liturgy.</note>

Seven women shall take hold **let Isaiah be read** of one man on that day, saying: "We will eat our own bread and be covered in our own clothes; only..." <note>The text here begins the reading of Isaiah 4:1. The instruction "let Isaiah be read" is a rubric (a liturgical direction) inserted directly into the start of the biblical verse.</note>

<summary>This manuscript page records a liturgical prayer linking the biblical Exodus to the sacrament of baptism, followed by procedural instructions for the ordination of readers and the start of a reading from the Prophet Isaiah.</summary>
<keywords>liturgy, baptism, ordination, Isaiah, Exodus, Readers, clergy, medieval prayer</keywords>
